truszczynski12a is stated for a set of formulas, which can be understood as an infinitary conjunction.

Thus,
I
is a stable model of
P
iff
I
is a stable model of
F
.

Second, we consider the case
I\not\models F
and show that
I
is neither a stable model of
F
nor
P
.
Proposition 1 by
\citeN
truszczynski12a states that
I
is a model of
F
iff
I
is a model of
F^{I}
.
Thus,
I
is not a stable model of
F
.
Furthermore, because
I\not\models F
, there is a rule
r\in P
such that
I\not\models B(r)\rightarrow H(r)
.
Consequently, we have
I\models B(r)
and
I\not\models H(r)
.
Using the above proposition again, we get
I\models B(r)^{I}
.
Because
I\models B(r)^{I}
and
I\not\models H(r)
, we get
I\not\models r^{I}
and in turn
I\not\models P^{I}
.
Thus,
I
is not a stable model of
P
either.
∎

Lemma 42
.

Let
F
be a formula, and
I
and
J
be interpretations.

If
F
is positive and
I\subseteq J
, then
I\models F
implies
J\models F
.

Proof.

This property can be shown by induction over the rank of the formula.
∎

The following two propositions shed some light on the two types of reducts.

Lemma 43
.

Let
F
be a formula, and
I
and
J
be interpretations.

Then,

(a)

if
F
is positive then
F^{I}
is positive,

(b)

I\models F
iff
I\models F^{I}
,

(c)

if
F
is strictly positive and
I\subseteq J
then
I\models F
iff
I\models F^{J}
.

Proof.

Property
(a)
.

Because the reduct only replaces subformulas by
\bot
, the resulting formula is still positive.

Property
(b)
.

Corresponds to Proposition 1 by
\citeN
truszczynski12a.

Property
(c)
.

This property can be shown by induction over the rank of the formula.∎

Lemma 44
.

Let
F
be a formula, and
I
,
J
, and
X
be interpretations.

Then,

(a)

F_{I}
is positive,

(b)

I\models F
iff
I\models F_{I}
,

(c)

if
F
is positive then
F=F_{I}
, and

(d)

if
I\subseteq J
then
X\models F_{J}
implies
X\models F_{I}
.

Proof.

Property
(a)
.

Because the
id
-reduct replaces all negative occurrences of atoms, the resulting formula is positive.

Property
(b)
.

This property holds
because when the reduct replaces an atom
a
, it is replaced by either
\top
or
\bot
depending on whether
I\models a
or
I\not\models a
.
This does not change the satisfaction of the subformula w.r.t.
I
.

Property
(c)
.

Because a positive formula does not contain negative occurrences of atoms, it is not changed by the
id
-reduct.

Property
(d)
.

We prove by induction over the rank of formula
F
that

\displaystyle X\models F_{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{I}\text{\ and}

(23)

\displaystyle X\models F_{\overline{I}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{\overline{J}}.

(24)

Base.

We consider the case that
F
is a formula of rank
0
.

F
is a formula of rank
0

implies
F
is an atom.

First, we show (
23
).
We assume
X\models F_{J}
:

F
is an atom

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F_{I}=F_{J}=F.

\displaystyle F_{I}=F_{J}\text{\ and\ }X\models F_{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{I}.

Second, we show (
24
).
We assume
X\models F_{\overline{I}}
:

\displaystyle\text{$F$ is an atom}\text{\ and\ }X\models F_{\overline{I}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F_{\overline{I}}=\top.

\displaystyle F_{\overline{I}}=\top

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F\in I.

\displaystyle F\in I\text{\ and\ }I\subseteq J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F\in J.

\displaystyle\text{$F$ is an atom}\text{\ and\ }F\in J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F_{\overline{J}}=\top.

\displaystyle F_{\overline{J}}=\top

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{\overline{J}}.

Hypothesis.

We assume that (
23
) and (
24
)
hold for formulas
F
of ranks smaller than
i
.

Step.

We only show (
23
) because (
24
) can be shown in a similar way.
We consider formulas
F
of rank
i
.

First, we consider the case that
F
is a conjunction of form
\mathcal{H}^{\wedge}
.

\displaystyle X\models\mathcal{H}^{\wedge}_{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models G_{J}\text{\ for all\ }G\in\mathcal{H}.

\displaystyle X\models G_{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models G_{I}\text{\ by\ hypothesis}.

\displaystyle X\models G_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models\mathcal{H}^{\wedge}_{I}.

The case for disjunctions can be proven in a similar way.
Last, we consider the case that
F
is an implication of form
G\rightarrow H
.
Observe that

\displaystyle F_{I}

\displaystyle=G_{\overline{I}}\rightarrow H_{I}\text{\ and}

\displaystyle F_{J}

\displaystyle=G_{\overline{J}}\rightarrow H_{J}.

First, we consider the case
X\not\models G_{\overline{J}}
:

\displaystyle X\not\models G_{\overline{J}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\not\models G_{\overline{I}}\text{\ by\ hypothesis}.

\displaystyle X\not\models G_{\overline{I}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{I}.

Second, we consider the case
X\models H_{J}
:

\displaystyle X\models H_{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models H_{I}\text{\ by\ hypothesis}.

\displaystyle X\models H_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models F_{I}.\qed

Proof of
Lemma

6
.

This lemma follows from Proposition 14 by
\citeN
dematr00a observing that the well-founded operator is a monotone symmetric operator.
The proposition is actually a bit more general stating that the operator maps any consistent four-valued interpretation to a consistent four-valued interpretation.
∎

Lemma 45
.

Let
O
and
O^{\prime}
be monotone operators over complete lattice
(L,\leq)
with
O^{\prime}(x)\leq O(x)
for each
x\in L
.

Then, we get
x^{\prime}\leq x
where
x^{\prime}
and
x
are the least fixed points of
O^{\prime}
and
O
, respectively.

Proof.

Let
y
be a prefixed point of
O
.
We have
O(y)\leq y
.
Because
O^{\prime}(y)\leq O(y)
, we get
O^{\prime}(y)\leq y
.
So each prefixed point of
O
is also a prefixed point of
O^{\prime}
.

Let
S^{\prime}
and
S
be the set of all prefixed points of
O^{\prime}
and
O
, respectively.
We obtain
S\subseteq S^{\prime}
.
By
Theorem

1

(a)
, we get that
x^{\prime}
is the greatest lower bound of
S^{\prime}
.
Observe that
x^{\prime}
is a lower bound for
S
.
By construction of
S
, we have
x\in S
.
Hence, we get
x^{\prime}\leq x
.
∎

Lemma 46
.

Let
P
and
P^{\prime}
be
\mathcal{F}
-programs and
I
be an interpretation.

Then,
P^{\prime}\subseteq P
implies
S_{P^{\prime}}(I)\subseteq S_{P}(I)
.

Proof.

This lemma is a direct consequence of
Lemma

45

observing that the one-step provability operator derives fewer consequences for
P^{\prime}
.
∎

Lemma 47
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{R}
-program,

I
be a two-valued interpretation, and

J=S_{P}(I)
.

Then,
X
is a stable model of
P
,
I\subseteq X
, and
I\subseteq J
implies
X\subseteq J
.

Proof.

Because
X
is a stable model of
P
, it is the only minimal model of
P^{X}
.
Furthermore, we have that
J
is a model of
P_{I}
.
To show that
X\subseteq J
, we show that
J
is also a model of
P^{X}
.
For this, it is enough to show that for each rule
r\in P
we have
J\not\models B(r)_{I}
implies
J\not\models B(r)^{X}
.
We prove inductively over the rank of the formula
F=B(r)
that
J\not\models F_{I}
implies
J\not\models F^{X}
.

Base.

We consider the case that
F
is a formula of rank
0
.

If
X\not\models F
, we get
J\not\models F^{X}
because
F^{X}=\bot
.
Thus, we only have to consider the case
X\models F
:

F
is a formula of rank
0

implies
F
is an atom.

F
is an atom

implies
F_{I}=F
.

\displaystyle X\models F\text{\ and\ }\text{$F$ is an atom}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F^{X}=F.

\displaystyle F_{I}=F\text{\ and\ }F^{X}=F

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F_{I}=F^{X}.

\displaystyle J\not\models F_{I}\text{\ and\ }F_{I}=F^{X}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models F^{X}.

Hypothesis.

We assume that
J\not\models F_{I}
implies
J\not\models F^{X}
holds for formulas
F
of ranks smaller than
i
.

Step.

We consider the case that
F
is a formula of rank
i
.

As in the base case, we only have to consider the case
X\models F
.
Furthermore, we have to distinguish the cases that
F
is a conjunction, disjunction, or implication.

We first consider the case that
F
is a conjunction of form
\mathcal{F}^{\wedge}
:

\displaystyle X\models F

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F^{X}=\{{G^{X}\mid G\in\mathcal{F}\}}^{\wedge}.

\displaystyle J\not\models F_{I}\text{\ and\ }F_{I}={\{G_{I}\mid G\in\mathcal{F}\}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models G_{I}\text{\ for some\ }G\in\mathcal{F}.

\displaystyle G\in\mathcal{F}\text{\ and\ }\text{$F$ has rank $i$}

implies
G
has rank less than
i
.

\displaystyle J\not\models G_{I}\text{\ and\ }\text{$G$ has rank less than $i$}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models G^{X}\text{\ by\ hypothesis}.

\displaystyle J\not\models G^{X}\text{\ and\ }F^{X}=\{{G^{X}\mid G\in F\}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models F^{X}.

The case that
F
is a disjunction can be shown in a similar way to the case that
F
is a conjunction.

Last, we consider the case that
F
is an implication of form
G\rightarrow H
.
Observe that
G
is positive because
F
has no occurrences of implications in its antecedent
and, furthermore, given that
F
is a formula of rank
i
,
H
is a formula of rank less than
i
.

We show
I\models G
:

\displaystyle J\not\models F_{I}\text{\ and\ }F_{I}=G_{\overline{I}}\rightarrow H_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\models G_{\overline{I}}

\displaystyle J\models G_{\overline{I}}\text{\ and\ }\text{$G$ is positive}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models G\text{\ because\ }G_{\overline{I}}\equiv\top.

We show
J\models G^{X}
:

\displaystyle\text{$G$ is positive}\text{,\ }I\subseteq X\text{, and\ }I\models G

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models G\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:monotone-positive}.}

\displaystyle X\models F\text{,\ }X\models G\text{, and\ }F=G\rightarrow H

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models H\text{.}

\displaystyle\text{$G$ is positive}\text{,\ }I\subseteq X\text{, and\ }I\models G

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models G^{X}\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:ferraris-basic}~\ref{lem:ferraris-basic:d}.}

G
is positive

implies
G^{X}
is positive
by
Lemma

43

(a)
.

\displaystyle G^{X}\text{\ is positive}\text{,\ }I\subseteq J\text{, and\ }I\models G^{X}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\models G^{X}\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:monotone-positive}.}

We show
J\not\models H^{X}
:

\displaystyle I\models G\text{\ and\ }F_{I}=G_{\overline{I}}\rightarrow H_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }F_{I}\equiv H_{I}\text{\ because\ }G_{\overline{I}}\equiv\top.

\displaystyle F_{I}\equiv H_{I}\text{\ and\ }J\not\models F_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models H_{I}.

\displaystyle J\not\models H_{I}\text{\ and\ $H$ has rank less than $i$}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models H^{X}\text{\ by hypothesis.}

Because
X\models F
, we have
F^{X}=G^{X}\rightarrow H^{X}
.
Using
J\models G^{X}
and
J\not\models H^{X}
, we get
J\not\models F^{X}
.∎

Proof of
Theorem

7
.

Let
X
be a stable model of
P
.

We prove by transfinite induction over the sequence of postfixed points leading to the well-founded model:

\displaystyle(I_{0},J_{0})

\displaystyle=(\emptyset,\Sigma),

\displaystyle(I_{\alpha+1},J_{\alpha+1})

\displaystyle=W_{P}(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})\text{\ for ordinals $\alpha$},\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle(I_{\beta},J_{\beta})

\displaystyle=(\bigcup_{\alpha<\beta}I_{\alpha},\bigcap_{\alpha<\beta}J_{\alpha})\text{\ for limit ordinals $\beta$.}

We have that
\alpha<\beta
implies
(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})\leq_{p}(I_{\beta},J_{\beta})
for ordinals
\alpha
and
\beta
,

I_{\alpha}\subseteq J_{\alpha}
for ordinals
\alpha
, and
there is a least ordinal
\alpha
such that
(I,J)=(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})
.

Base.

We have
I_{0}\subseteq X\subseteq J_{0}
.

Hypothesis.

We assume
I_{\beta}\subseteq X\subseteq J_{\beta}
for all ordinals
\beta<\alpha
.

Step.

If
\alpha=\beta+1
is a successor ordinal we have

\displaystyle(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=W_{P}(I_{\beta},J_{\beta})

\displaystyle=(S_{P}(J_{\beta}),S_{P}(I_{\beta})).

By the induction hypothesis we have
I_{\beta}\subseteq X\subseteq J_{\beta}
.

First, we show
I_{\alpha}\subseteq X
:

X
is a (stable) model

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P}(X)\subseteq X.

\displaystyle X\subseteq J_{\beta}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P}(J_{\beta})\subseteq S_{P}(X).

\displaystyle S_{P}(X)\subseteq X\text{\ and\ }S_{P}(J_{\beta})\subseteq S_{P}(X)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P}(J_{\beta})\subseteq X.

\displaystyle I_{\alpha}=S_{P}(J_{\beta})\text{\ and\ }S_{P}(J_{\beta})\subseteq X

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I_{\alpha}\subseteq X.

Second, we show
X\subseteq J_{\alpha}
:

\displaystyle\beta<\alpha

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I_{\beta},J_{\beta})\leq_{p}(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})

\displaystyle(I_{\beta},J_{\beta})\leq_{p}(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})\text{\ and\ }I_{\alpha}\subseteq J_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I_{\beta}\subseteq J_{\alpha}

X
is a stable model,
I_{\beta}\subseteq X
,

J_{\alpha}=S_{P}(I_{\beta})
, and
I_{\beta}\subseteq J_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\text{$X\subseteq J_{\alpha}$ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:stable:possible}}.

We have shown
I_{\alpha}\subseteq X\subseteq J_{\alpha}
for successor ordinals.

If
\alpha
is a limit ordinal we have

\displaystyle(I_{\alpha},J_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=(\bigcup_{\beta<\alpha}I_{\beta},\bigcap_{\beta<\alpha}J_{\beta}).

Let
x\in I_{\alpha}
.
There must be an ordinal
\beta<\alpha
such that
x\in I_{\beta}
.
Since
I_{\beta}\subseteq X
by the hypothesis, we have
x\in X
.
Thus,
I_{\alpha}\subseteq X
.

Let
x\in X
.
For each ordinal
\beta<\alpha
we have
x\in J_{\beta}
because
X\subseteq J_{\beta}
by the hypothesis.
Thus, we get
x\in J_{\alpha}
.
It follows that
X\subseteq J_{\alpha}
.

We have shown
I_{\alpha}\subseteq X\subseteq J_{\alpha}
for limit ordinals.∎

Lemma 48
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program and
(I,J)
be a four-valued interpretation.

Then, we have
H(P^{I,J})=T_{P_{I}}(J)
.

Proof.

The program
P^{I,J}
contains all rules
r\in P
such that
J\models B(r)_{I}
.
This are exactly the rules whose heads are gathered by the
T
operator.
∎

Lemma 49
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program and
(I,J)
be the well-founded model of
P
.

Then, we have

(a)

S_{P^{I,J}}(I^{\prime})=J
for all
I^{\prime}\subseteq I
, and

(b)

S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime})=S_{P}(J^{\prime})
for all
J\subseteq J^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we use

\displaystyle S_{P}(J)

\displaystyle=I,

\displaystyle S_{P}(I)

\displaystyle=J,

\displaystyle P^{I,J}

\displaystyle\subseteq P\text{, and}

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle\subseteq J\text{\ because the well-founded model is consistent.}

Property
(a)
.

We show
J=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)
.
Let
\hat{J}=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)
and
r\in P\setminus P^{I,J}
:

\displaystyle P^{I,J}\subseteq P

and

\displaystyle\hat{J}=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)

and

\displaystyle J=S_{P}(I)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\hat{J}\subseteq J\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:program:positive:subset}.}

\displaystyle r\notin P^{I,J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models B(r)_{I}.

\displaystyle\hat{J}\subseteq J\text{\ and\ }J\not\models B(r)_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\hat{J}\not\models B(r)_{I}\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:monotone-positive}.}

\displaystyle\hat{J}=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)\text{\ and\ }\hat{J}\not\models B(r)_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\hat{J}\models P_{I}.

\displaystyle\hat{J}\models P_{I}\text{\ and\ }J=S_{P}(I)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\subseteq\hat{J}.

\displaystyle J\subseteq\hat{J}\text{\ and\ }\hat{J}\subseteq J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J=\hat{J}.

Thus, we get that
S_{P^{I,J}}(I)=J
.

With this we can continue to prove
S_{P^{I,J}}(I^{\prime})=J
.
Let
r\in P^{I,J}
:

\displaystyle r\in P^{I,J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\models B(r)_{I}.

\displaystyle r\in P^{I,J}\text{\ and\ }P^{I,J}\subseteq P

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }r\in P.

\displaystyle J\models B(r)_{I}\text{,\ }r\in P,\text{\ and\ }S_{P}(I)=J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }H(r)\in J.

\displaystyle J\models B(r)_{I}\text{\ and\ }I^{\prime}\subseteq I

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\models B(r)_{I^{\prime}}\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:foid-basic}~\ref{lem:foid-basic:d}.}

\displaystyle H(r)\in J\text{\ and\ }J\models B(r)_{I^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P^{I,J}}(I^{\prime})\subseteq J.

\displaystyle I^{\prime}\subseteq I\text{\ and\ }J=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\subseteq S_{P^{I,J}}(I^{\prime}).

Thus, we get
S_{P^{I,J}}(I^{\prime})=J
.

Property
(b)
.

Let
I^{\prime}=S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime})
and
r\in P\setminus P^{I,J}
:

\displaystyle r\notin P^{I,J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models B(r)_{I}.

\displaystyle I\subseteq J\text{,\ }J\subseteq J^{\prime}\text{, and\ }J\not\models B(r)_{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\not\models B(r)_{J^{\prime}}.\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:foid-basic}~\ref{lem:foid-basic:d}.}.

\displaystyle I^{\prime}\subseteq I\text{,\ }I\subseteq J\text{, and\ }J\not\models B(r)_{J^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I^{\prime}\not\models B(r)_{J^{\prime}}.\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:monotone-positive}.}

\displaystyle I^{\prime}=S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime})\text{\ and\ }I^{\prime}\not\models B(r)_{J^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P}(J^{\prime})\subseteq S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime}).

\displaystyle P^{I,J}\subseteq P

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime})\subseteq S_{P}(J^{\prime})\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:program:positive:subset}.}

Thus, we get
S_{P^{I,J}}(J^{\prime})=S_{P}(J^{\prime})
.∎

Proof of
Theorem

8
.

By
Lemma

49
, we have
(I,J)=W_{P^{I,J}}(I,J)
.
Furthermore, we let
(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})=\mathit{WM}(P^{I,J})
:

\displaystyle(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})=\mathit{WM}(P^{I,J})\text{\ and\ }(I,J)=W_{P^{I,J}}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(I,J).

by
Theorem

1

(c)
.

\displaystyle\widehat{I}\subseteq I

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P^{I,J}}(\widehat{I})=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)

by
Lemma

49

(a)
.

\displaystyle\hat{J}=S_{P^{I,J}}(\widehat{I})=S_{P^{I,J}}(I)=J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widehat{J}=J.

\displaystyle\widehat{I}=S_{P^{I,J}}(\widehat{J})\text{,\ }S_{P^{I,J}}(J)=I\text{, and\ }\widehat{J}=J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widehat{I}=I.

We obtain
(I,J)=(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

9
.

We first show that all rule bodies removed by the simplification are falsified by
X
.
Let
r\in P\setminus P^{I,J}
and assume
X\models{B(r)}
:

\displaystyle X\models B(r)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models B(r)_{X}\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:foid-basic}~\ref{lem:foid-basic:b}}.

\displaystyle X\models B(r)_{X}\text{\ and\ }I\subseteq X

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }X\models B(r)_{I}\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:foid-basic}~\ref{lem:foid-basic:d}}.

\displaystyle X\models B(r)_{I}\text{\ and\ }X\subseteq J

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }J\models B(r)_{I}\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{lem:monotone-positive}}.

This is a contradiction and, thus,
X\not\models B(r)
.
We use the following consequence in the proof below:

\displaystyle X\not\models B(r)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }{(P\setminus P^{I,J})}^{X}\equiv\emptyset.

To show the theorem, we show that
P^{X}
and
{(P^{I,J})}^{X}
have the same minimal models.
Clearly, we have
P^{X}={(P^{I,J})}^{X}\cup{(P\setminus P^{I,J})}^{X}
.
Using this and
{(P\setminus P^{I,J})}^{X}\equiv\emptyset
,
we obtain that
P^{X}
and
{(P^{I,J})}^{X}
have the same minimal models.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

10
.

The result follows from
Theorems

9
,
8
and
7
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

11
.

By
Lemma

46
,
we have
S_{P^{I,J}}(X)\subseteq S_{Q}(X)\subseteq S_{P}(X)
for any two-valued interpretation
X
.
Thus, by
Theorem

8
, we get
(I,J)=W_{Q}(I,J)
.

Let
(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})
be a prefixed point of
W_{Q}
with
(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)
.
We have
(S_{Q}(\widehat{J}),S_{Q}(\widehat{I}))\leq_{p}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)
.

\displaystyle J\subseteq\widehat{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P^{I,J}}(\widehat{J})=S_{Q}(\widehat{J})=S_{P}(\widehat{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{prp:simplification:well-founded}~\ref{prp:simplification:well-founded:b}}.

\displaystyle S_{Q}(\widehat{J})=S_{P}(\widehat{J})\text{\ and\ }S_{Q}(\widehat{J})\subseteq\widehat{I}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }S_{P}(\widehat{J})\subseteq\widehat{I}.

\displaystyle\widehat{J}\subseteq S_{Q}(\widehat{I})\text{\ and\ }S_{Q}(\widehat{I})\subseteq S_{P}(\widehat{I})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widehat{J}\subseteq S_{P}(\widehat{I}).

\displaystyle S_{P}(\widehat{J})\subseteq\widehat{I}\text{\ and\ }\widehat{J}\subseteq S_{P}(\widehat{I})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }W_{P}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J}).

\displaystyle W_{P}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)\leq_{p}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:knaster-tarski}~\ref{thm:knaster-tarski:a}}.

\displaystyle(I,J)\leq_{p}(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\text{\ and\ }(\widehat{I},\widehat{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)=(\widehat{I},\widehat{J}).

By
Theorem

1

(a)
, we obtain that
\mathit{WM}(Q)=(I,J)
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

12
.

Observe that
P^{I,J}=Q^{I,J}
.
With this, the corollary follows from
Corollaries

10
and
11
.
∎

Alternatively,
the incorporation of context atoms can also be seen as a form of partial evaluation applied to the underlying program.

Definition 22
.

Let
\mathit{I\!C}
be a two-valued interpretation.

We define the
partial evaluation
of an
\mathcal{F}
-formula w.r.t.
\mathit{I\!C}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({a})

\displaystyle=\top\text{\ if }a\in\mathit{I\!C}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({a})

\displaystyle=a

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({a})

\displaystyle=a\text{\ if }a\notin\mathit{I\!C}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({\mathcal{H}^{\wedge}})

\displaystyle={\{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({F})\mid F\in\mathcal{H}\}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({\mathcal{H}^{\wedge}})

\displaystyle={\{\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({F})\mid F\in\mathcal{H}\}}^{\wedge}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({\mathcal{H}^{\vee}})

\displaystyle={\{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({F})\mid F\in\mathcal{H}\}}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({\mathcal{H}^{\vee}})

\displaystyle={\{\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({F})\mid F\in\mathcal{H}\}}^{\vee}

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({F\rightarrow G})

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({F})\rightarrow\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({G})

\displaystyle\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({F\rightarrow G})

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({F})\rightarrow\mathit{pe}_{\overline{\mathit{I\!C}}}({G})

where
a
is an atom,
\mathcal{H}
a set of formulas, and
F
and
G
are formulas.

The partial evaluation of an
\mathcal{F}
-program
P
w.r.t. a two-valued interpretation
\mathit{I\!C}
is

\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})=\{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({r})\mid r\in P\}
where

\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({r})=H(h)\leftarrow\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({B(r)})
.
Accordingly,
the partial evaluation of rules boils down to replacing satisfied
positive occurrences of atoms in rule bodies by
\top
.

We observe the following relationship between the relative one-step operators and partial evaluations.

Observation 50
.

Let
P
be a positive
\mathcal{F}
-program and
\mathit{I\!C}
be a two-valued interpretation.

Then, we have for any two-valued interpretation
I
that

\displaystyle T_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(I)

\displaystyle=T_{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})}(I).

Note that
{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})_{J}}={\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P_{J}})}
.

Proof of
Proposition

13
.

Clearly,
\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})
is positive whenever
P
is positive.
Using
50
,
we obtain that
T_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}
is monotone.

The second property directly follows from the monotonicity of the one-step provability operator.
∎

Lemma 51
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program and
\mathit{I\!C}
be a two-valued interpretation.

For any two-valued interpretation
J
,
we get

\displaystyle S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)

\displaystyle=\mathit{LM}(\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})_{J}).

Proof.

This lemma immediately follows from
50
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

14
.

Both properties can be shown by inspecting the reduced programs.

Property
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
implies
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J^{\prime})
.

Observe that we can use
Lemma

51
to equivalently write
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)=S_{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})}(J)
and
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J^{\prime})=S_{\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})}(J^{\prime})
.
With this and
Proposition

4
, we see that the relative stable operator is antimonotone just as the stable operator.

Property
\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{I\!C}
implies
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}(J)\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)
.

Observe that
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)
is equal to the least fixed point of
T_{P_{J}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}

and
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}(J)
is equal to the least fixed point of
T_{P_{J}}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}
.
Furthermore, observe that
T_{P_{J}}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}(X)\subseteq T_{P_{J}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(X)
for any two-valued interpretation
X
because
\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{I\!C}
and the underlying
T
operator is monotone.
With this and
Lemma

45
, we have shown the property.∎

Observation 52
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program, and
\mathit{I\!C}
and
J
be two-valued interpretations.

We get the following properties:

(a)

S_{P}^{\emptyset}(J)=S_{P}(J)
,

(b)

S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)\subseteq H(P)
, and

(c)

S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)=S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cap{B(P)}^{+}}(J\cap{B(P)}^{-})
.

Proof of
Proposition

15
.

Both properties can be shown by using the monotonicity of the underlying relative stable operator:

Property
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})\leq_{p}(I,J)
implies
W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})\leq_{p}W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(I,J)
.

Given that
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}
is antimonotone and
J^{\prime}\cup\mathit{J\!C}\subseteq J\cup\mathit{J\!C}
,
we have
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C})\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J^{\prime}\cup\mathit{J\!C})
.
Analogously, we can show
S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I^{\prime}\cup\mathit{I\!C})\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C})
.
We get
(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C}),S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C}))\leq_{p}(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J^{\prime}\cup\mathit{J\!C}),S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I^{\prime}\cup\mathit{I\!C}))
.

Hence,
W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}
is monotone.

Property
(\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})
implies
W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}}(I,J)\leq_{p}W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(I,J)
.

We have to show
(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}),S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}))\leq_{p}(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C}),\allowbreak S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C}))
.

Given that
\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{I\!C}
and
J\cup\mathit{J\!C}\subseteq J\cup\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}
, we obtain
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime})\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J\cup\mathit{J\!C})
using
Proposition

14
.
The same argument can be used for the possible atoms of the four-valued interpretations.
Given that
\mathit{J\!C}\subseteq\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}
and
I\cup\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime}\subseteq I\cup\mathit{I\!C}
, we obtain
S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C})\subseteq S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}}(I\cup\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime})
using
Proposition

14
.

Hence, we have shown
W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}^{\prime}}(I,J)\leq_{p}W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(I,J)
.∎

Observation 53
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program, and
I
,
I^{\prime}
and
\mathit{I\!C}
be two-valued interpretations.

We get the following properties:

(a)

I\models P
and
\mathit{I\!C}\subseteq I
implies
I\models\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})
,

(b)

I\models\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})
and
I^{\prime}\cap B(P)^{+}\subseteq\mathit{I\!C}
implies
I\cup I^{\prime}\models\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})
, and

(c)

I\models\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({P})
implies
I\models P
.

Lemma 54
.

Let
\mathit{P\!B}
and
\mathit{P\!T}
be
\mathcal{F}
-programs,

\mathit{I\!C}
and
J
be two-valued interpretations,

I=S_{\mathit{P\!B}\cup\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)
,

\mathit{I\!E}=I\cap({B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{+}\cap H(\mathit{P\!T}))
,

\mathit{I\!B}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(J)
, and

{\mathit{I\!T}}=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!B}}(J)
.

Then, we have
I=\mathit{I\!B}\cup{\mathit{I\!T}}
.

Proof.

Let
\widetilde{I}=\mathit{I\!B}\cup{\mathit{I\!T}}
.
Furthermore, we use the following programs:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({\mathit{P\!B}_{J}})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}({\mathit{P\!B}_{J}})=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!E}}({\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}})

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}}({\mathit{P\!T}_{J}})

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}

\displaystyle=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!B}}({\mathit{P\!T}_{J}})=\mathit{pe}_{\mathit{I\!B}}({\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}})

Observe that

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle=S_{\mathit{P\!B}\cup\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(J)=\mathit{LM}(\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}\cup\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}),

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!B}

\displaystyle=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(J)=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}})\text{, and}

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}

\displaystyle=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!B}}(J)=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}).

To show that
\widetilde{I}\subseteq I
, we show that
I
is a model of both
\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}
and
\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}
.
To show that
I\subseteq\widetilde{I}
, we show that
\widetilde{I}
is a model of both
\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}
and
\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}
.

Property
I\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}
.

\displaystyle I=\mathit{LM}(\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}\cup\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}.

\displaystyle I\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!E}\subseteq I

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:a}}.

Property
I\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}
.

\displaystyle I=\mathit{LM}(\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}\cup\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}.

\displaystyle I\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!B}=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\mathit{I\!B}\subseteq I.

\displaystyle I\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!B}\subseteq I

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}.

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:a}}.

Property
\widetilde{I}\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}
.

Let
E={B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{+}\cap H(\mathit{P\!T})
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}\subseteq I\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!E}=I\cap E

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\mathit{I\!T}\cap E\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}.

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\mathit{I\!T}\subseteq H(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}).

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}\cap E\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!T}\subseteq H(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\mathit{I\!T}\cap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{+}\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}.

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}\cap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{+}\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\mathit{I\!T}\cap{B(\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}})}^{+}\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}.

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}\cap{B(\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}})}^{+}\subseteq\mathit{I\!E}\text{\ and\ }\mathit{I\!B}=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widetilde{I}\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:b}}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widetilde{I}\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!B}}

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:c}}.

Property
\widetilde{I}\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}
.

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!T}=\mathit{LM}(\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widetilde{I}\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:b}}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{I}\models\widetilde{\mathit{P\!T}}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widetilde{I}\models\widehat{\mathit{P\!T}}

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:evaluation:properties}~\ref{obs:evaluation:properties:c}}.\qed

Proof of
Theorem

16
.

Let
P=\mathit{P\!B}\cup\mathit{P\!T}
and
E={B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\cap H(\mathit{P\!T})
.
We begin by evaluating
P
,
\mathit{P\!B}
and
\mathit{P\!T}
w.r.t.
(I,J)
and obtain

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle=W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(I,J)

\displaystyle=(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J),S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I)),

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}(I,J)

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}\cup J),S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}\cup I))\text{, and}

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})}(I,J)

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}}\cup J),S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}}\cup I)).

Using
(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqsubseteq(I,J)
, we get

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J),S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I)).

By
Lemma

54
and
52

(c)
, we get

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle\sqsubseteq(I,J),

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J),S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I))\text{, and}

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}}).

We first show
(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})
and then
({\mathit{I\!T}},{\mathit{J\!T}})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})
.

Property
(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})
.

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqsubseteq(I,J)

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})=(I,J)\sqcap E

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqsubseteq(I,J).

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqsubseteq(I,J).

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqsubseteq H(\mathit{P\!T})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap E.

(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap E\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqsubseteq(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}).

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

and

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}).

With the above, we use
52

(c)
to show that
(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})
is a fixed point of
W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{J\!C},\mathit{J\!E})}
:

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}(I,J)

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}((I,J)\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}((\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}))

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

Thus, by
Theorem

1

(c)
,
(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})
.

Property
(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})
.

To show the property, let

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

\displaystyle=(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}}),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

\displaystyle=W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\sqcap E,

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{J}),S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{I})),

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle=(S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{J}),S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{I})),\text{\ and}

\displaystyle W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\text{\ by \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prp:splitting:stable}.}

We get:

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J).

\displaystyle W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}).

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}\cup\mathit{J\!B})

\displaystyle\hbox to0.0pt{\text{\ and\ }\hss}\phantom{\text{\ implies\ }}\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}\cup\mathit{I\!B}).

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:stable:relative:properties}~\ref{obs:stable:relative:properties:c}}.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}\cup\mathit{J\!B})

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}\cup\mathit{I\!B})

and

\displaystyle\mathit{I\!B}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}\cup\mathit{J\!B})

and

\displaystyle\mathit{J\!B}=S_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!E}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\mathit{I\!E}\cup\mathit{I\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prp:well-founded:relative:monotonicity}}.

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}}).

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}}=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J)

and

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}}=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I)

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}}=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{J})

and

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}}=S_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{\mathit{J\!C}\cup\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup\widetilde{I})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftype\lx@tilde refnum}{prp:stable:relative:monotonicity}}.

\displaystyle W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\leq_{p}(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}}).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})\sqsubseteq W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

and

\displaystyle W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J}).

\displaystyle\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(P)=(I,J)

and

\displaystyle W_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)\leq_{p}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{thm:knaster-tarski}~\ref{thm:knaster-tarski:a}}.

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\leq_{p}(I,J)\text{\ and\ }(I,J)\leq_{p}(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)=(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J}).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J}).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})=(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

and

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E}).

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{I},\widetilde{J})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E},\mathit{J\!E})}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:stable:relative:properties}~\ref{obs:stable:relative:properties:c}}.

Property
(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})=({\mathit{I\!T}},{\mathit{J\!T}})
.

Observe that the lemma can be applied with
\mathit{P\!B}
and
\mathit{P\!T}
exchanged.
Let

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}

\displaystyle={B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\cap H(\mathit{P\!B})\text{,}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

\displaystyle=(I,J)\cap\widetilde{E}\text{,}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle=\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})}(\mathit{P\!T})\text{, and}

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle=W_{\mathit{P\!B}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})}(I,J).

Using the properties shown so far, we obtain

\displaystyle(I,J)

\displaystyle=(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}}).

With this we get:

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})

and

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!B}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcup(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqsubseteq H(\mathit{P\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})=(I,J)\sqcap\widetilde{E}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}).

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqsubseteq H(\mathit{P\!B})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})=(I,J)\sqcap\widetilde{E}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}).

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

and

\displaystyle(I,J)=(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!B}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!B}})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(I,J)\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}}).

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

and

\displaystyle(I,J)\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!B})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})=W_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})}(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})

and

\displaystyle(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})=W_{\mathit{P\!T}}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})}(I,J)

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})=(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:stable:relative:properties}~\ref{obs:stable:relative:properties:c}}.

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})\sqcap{B(\mathit{P\!T})}^{\pm}\sqsubseteq(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})

and

\displaystyle(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})=\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!E}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!E}})}(\mathit{P\!T})

and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!T},\mathit{J\!T})=\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!B},\mathit{J\!B})}(\mathit{P\!T})

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }(\widetilde{\mathit{I\!T}},\widetilde{\mathit{J\!T}})=(\mathit{I\!T},\mathit{J\!T})

\displaystyle\text{\ by\ \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{obs:stable:relative:properties}~\ref{obs:stable:relative:properties:c}}.

Thus, we get
(\widehat{\mathit{I\!T}},\widehat{\mathit{J\!T}})=({\mathit{I\!T}},{\mathit{J\!T}})
.∎

Proof of
Theorem

17
.

The theorem can be shown by transfinite induction over the sequence indices.
We do not give the full induction proof here but focus on the key idea.
Let
({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})
be the intermediate interpretations as in
Equation

5

when computing the well-founded model of the sequence.
Furthermore, let

\displaystyle(I_{i},J_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(\mathit{I\!E}_{i},\mathit{J\!E}_{i})}({P}_{i})

be the intermediate interpretations
where
(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})
is the union of the intermediate interpretations as in
Equation

4
and

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!E}_{i},\mathit{J\!E}_{i})

\displaystyle=(I,J)\cap E_{i}

with
E_{i}
as in
Equation

3
.

Observe that with
Theorem

16
, we have

\mathit{WM}(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{P}_{i})=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}(I_{i},J_{i})
.
By
Proposition

15
, we have
({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})\leq_{p}(I_{i},J_{i})

and, thus, we obtain that
\mathit{WM}({({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}})\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{P}_{i})
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

18
.

By
Theorem

17
, we have

\displaystyle\bigsqcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}(I_{i},J_{i})

\displaystyle\leq_{p}(I,J).

We get
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}I_{i}\subseteq I
and, thus,

\displaystyle\bigcup_{i\leq k}I_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathit{I\!C}_{k}\cup I_{k}\subseteq I.

Using
J\subseteq\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}J_{i}
and
J_{i}\subseteq H({P}_{i})
, we get

\displaystyle J\cap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\subseteq(\bigcup_{i\leq k}J_{i}\cup\bigcup_{k<i}H({P}_{i}))\cap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\subseteq(\bigcup_{i\leq k}J_{i}\cup E_{k})\cap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\subseteq(\mathit{J\!C}_{k}\cup J_{k}\cup E_{k})\cap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}.

Using both results, we obtain

\displaystyle((\mathit{I\!C}_{k},\mathit{J\!C}_{k})\sqcup(\emptyset,E_{k})\sqcup(I_{k},J_{k}))\sqcap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}

\displaystyle\leq_{p}(I,J)\sqcap{B({P}_{k})}^{\pm}.

Because the body literals determine the simplification, we get

\displaystyle{P}_{k}^{I,J}

\displaystyle\subseteq{P}_{k}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{k},\mathit{J\!C}_{k})\sqcup(\emptyset,E_{k})\sqcup(I_{k},J_{k})}.\qed

Lemma 55
.

Let
{({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be a sequence of
\mathcal{R}
-programs,
and
(I,J)
be the well-founded model of
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{{P}_{i}}
.

Then,
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{{P}_{i}}
and
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{{Q}_{i}}

with
{P}_{i}^{I,J}\subseteq{Q}_{i}\subseteq{P}_{i}

have the same well-founded and stable models.

Proof.

This lemma is a direct consequence of
Theorems

11
,
12
and
18
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

19
.

This corollary is a direct consequence of
Theorems

18
and
55
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

20
.

This can be proven in the same way as
Theorem

17
but note that because
E_{i}
is empty,
we get
({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})=(I_{i},J_{i})
.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

21
.

This can be proven in the same way as
Theorem

18
but note that because
E_{i}
is empty,
all
\leq_{p}
and most
\subseteq
relations can be replaced with equivalences.
∎

Whenever head atoms do not interfere with negative body literals,
the relative well founded-model of a program can be calculated with just two applications of the relative stable operator.

Lemma 56
.

Let
P
be an
\mathcal{F}
-program
such that
{B(P)}^{-}\cap H(P)=\emptyset

and
(\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})
be a four-valued interpretation.

Then,
\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(P)=(S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}),S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}))
.

Proof.

Let
(I,J)=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}(P)
.

We have
J=S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I)
.
By
52

(b)
, we get
J\subseteq H(P)
.
With this and
{B(P)}^{-}\cap H(P)=\emptyset
, we get
{B(P)}^{-}\cap J=\emptyset
.
Thus,
S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J)=S_{P}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C})
by
52

(c)
.

The same arguments apply to show
S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I)=S_{P}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C})
.
∎

Any sequence as in
Corollary

20
in which
each
P_{i}
additionally satisfies the precondition of
Lemma

56

has a total well-founded model.
Furthermore, the well-founded model of such a sequence can be calculated
with just two (independent) applications of the relative stable operator per program
P_{i}
in the sequence.

Proof of
Proposition

23
.

We use
Lemma

41
to show that both formulas are strongly equivalent.

Property
I\models\pi(a)^{J}
implies
I\models\tau(a)^{J}
for arbitrary interpretations
I
.

The formulas
\pi(a)
and
\tau(a)
only differ in the consequents of their implications.
Observe that the consequents in
\pi(a)
are stronger than the ones in
\tau(a)
.
Thus, it follows that
\pi(a)
is stronger than
\tau(a)
.
Furthermore, observe that the same holds for their reducts.

Property
I\not\models\pi(a)^{J}
implies
I\not\models\tau(a)^{J}
for arbitrary interpretations
I
.

Let
G
be the set of all instance of the aggregate elements of
a
.
Because
I\not\models\pi(a)^{J}
,
there must be a set
D\subseteq G
such that

D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a
,
I\models{(\tau(D)^{\wedge})}^{J}
, and
I\not\models{({\pi_{a}}(D)^{\vee})}^{J}
.
With this, we construct the set

\displaystyle\widehat{D}=D\cup\{e\in G\setminus D\mid I\models\tau(e)^{J}\}.

The construction of
\widehat{D}

and

\displaystyle D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a

and

\displaystyle I\not\models{({\pi_{a}}(D)^{\vee})}^{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }\widehat{D}\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a\text{\ and\ }I\not\models{(\tau_{a}(\widehat{D})^{\vee})}^{J}.

The construction of
\widehat{D}

and

\displaystyle I\models{(\tau(D)^{\wedge})}^{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\models{(\tau(\widehat{D})^{\wedge})}^{J}.

\displaystyle\widehat{D}\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a

and

\displaystyle I\not\models{(\tau_{a}(\widehat{D})^{\vee})}^{J}

and

\displaystyle I\models{(\tau(\widehat{D})^{\wedge})}^{J}

\displaystyle\text{\ implies\ }I\not\models\tau(a)^{J}.\qed

Proof of
Proposition

24
.

Let
G
be the set of ground instances of the aggregate elements of
a
.
Furthermore, observe that a monotone aggregate
a
is either constantly true or not justified by the empty set.

In case that
\pi(a)\equiv\top
, we get
\pi(a)_{I}\equiv\top
and the lemma holds.

Next, we consider the case that the empty set does not justify the aggregate.
Observe that
{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset)
is stronger than
{\pi_{a}}(D)
for any
D\subseteq G
.
And, we have that
\pi(a)
contains the implication
\top\rightarrow{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset)
.
Because of this, we have
\pi(a)\equiv{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset)
.
Furthermore, all consequents in
\pi(a)
are positive formulas and, thus, not modified by the reduct.
Thus, the reduct
{(\top\rightarrow{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset))}_{I}
is equal to
\top\rightarrow{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset)
.
And as before, it is stronger than all other implications in
\pi(a)_{I}
.
Hence, we get
\pi(a)_{I}\equiv{\pi_{a}}(\emptyset)
.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

25
.

Remember that the translation
\pi(a)
is a conjunction of implications.
The antecedents of the implications are conjunctions of aggregate elements and the consequents are disjunctions of conjunctions of aggregate elements.

Property
(a)
.

If the conjunction in an antecedent contains an element not in
J
, then the conjunction is not satisfied by
X
and the implication does not affect the satisfiability of
\pi(a)
.
If a conjunction in a consequent contains an element not in
J
, then
X
does not satisfy the conjunction and the conjunction does not affect the satisfiability of the encompassing disjunction.
Observe that both cases correspond exactly to those subformulas omitted in
{\pi_{J}}(a)
.

The remaining two properties follow for similar reasons.
∎

The next observation summarizes how dependencies transfer from non-ground aggregate programs to the corresponding ground
\mathcal{R}
-programs.

Observation 57
.

Let
{P}_{1}
and
{P}_{2}
be aggregate programs, and
G_{1}=\pi(P_{1})
and
G_{2}=\pi(P_{2})
.

Then,

(a)

{P}_{1}
does not depend on
{P}_{2}
implies
{B({G}_{1})}^{\pm}\cap H({G}_{2})=\emptyset
,

(b)

{P}_{1}
does not positively depend on
{P}_{2}
implies
{B({G}_{1})}^{+}\cap H({G}_{2})=\emptyset
,

(c)

{P}_{1}
does not negatively depend on
{P}_{2}
implies
{B({G}_{1})}^{-}\cap H({G}_{2})=\emptyset
.

The next two lemmas pin down important properties of instantiation sequences.
First of all,
there are no external atoms in the components of instantiation sequences.

Lemma 58
.

Let
P
be an aggregate program and
{({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be an instantiation sequence for
P
.

Then, for the sequence
{({G}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
with
{G}_{i}=\pi({P}_{i})
,
we have
E_{i}=\emptyset
for each
i\in\mathbb{I}
where
E_{i}
is defined as in
Equation

3
.

Proof.

This lemma is a direct consequence of
57

(a)
and the anti-symmetry
of the dependency relation between components.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

26
.

This theorem is a direct consequence of
Lemmas

58
and
20
.
∎

Moreover, for each stratified component in an instantiation sequence, we obtain a total well-founded model.

Lemma 59
.

Let
P
be an aggregate program and
{({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
be an instantiation sequence for
P
.

Then, for the sequence
{({G}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
with
{G}_{i}=\pi({P}_{i})
,
we have
I_{i}=J_{i}=S_{{G}_{i}}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i}}(\mathit{I\!C}_{i})

for each stratified component
{P}_{i}
where

(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})
and
(I_{i},J_{i})
are defined as in
Equations

4
and
5

in the construction of the well-founded model of
{({G}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
in
Definition

6
.

Proof.

In the following, we use
E_{i}
and
(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})
for the sequence
{({G}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
as defined in
Equations

3
and
4
.
Note that, by
Lemma

58
, we have
E_{i}=\emptyset
.

We prove by induction.

Base.

Let
{P}_{i}
be a stratified component that does not depend on any other component.
Because
{P}_{i}
does not depend on any other component, we have
\bigcup_{j<i}H({G}_{j})\cap{B({G}_{i})}^{\pm}=\emptyset
.
Thus, by
52

(b)
, we get
\mathit{I\!C}_{i}\cap{B({G}_{i})}^{\pm}=\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cap{B({G}_{i})}^{\pm}=\emptyset
.
By
52

(c)
, we get
(I_{i},J_{i})=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i}}({G}_{i})=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{I\!C}_{i}}({G}_{i})
.
Because
{P}_{i}
is stratified, we have
{B({G}_{i})}^{-}\cap H({G}_{i})=\emptyset
.
We then use
Lemma

56
to obtain
I_{i}=J_{i}=S_{{G}_{i}}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i}}(\mathit{I\!C}_{i})
.

Hypothesis.

We assume that the theorem holds for any component
{P}_{j}
with
j<i
.

Step.

Let
{P}_{i}
be a stratified component.
For any
j<i
, component
{P}_{i}
either depends on
{P}_{j}
or not.
If
{P}_{i}
depends on
{P}_{j}
, then
{P}_{j}
is stratified and we get
I_{j}=J_{j}
by the induction hypothesis.
If
{P}_{i}
does not depend on
{P}_{j}
, then
I_{j}\cap{B({G}_{i})}^{\pm}=J_{j}\cap{B({G}_{i})}^{\pm}=\emptyset
.
By
52

(c)
, we get
(I_{i},J_{i})=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i}}({G}_{i})=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{I\!C}_{i}}({G}_{i})
.
Just as in the base case, by
Lemma

56
, we get
I_{i}=J_{i}=S_{{G}_{i}}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{i}}(\mathit{I\!C}_{i})
.∎

Lemma 60
.

Let
P
be an aggregate program and
{({P}_{i,j})}_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{J}}
be a refined instantiation sequence for
P
.

Then, for the sequence
{({G}_{i,j})}_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{J}}
with
{G}_{i,j}=\pi({P}_{i,j})
,
we have
E_{i,j}\cap{B({G}_{i,j})}^{+}=\emptyset
for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{J}

where
E_{i,j}
is defined as in
Equation

3
.

Proof.

The same arguments as in the proof of
Lemma

58
can be used but using
57

(b)
instead.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

27
.

Let
G=\pi(P)
,
G^{\prime}=\pi(P^{\prime})
with
P^{\prime}
as in
Definition

13
,
(I,J)=\mathit{AM}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}}_{E}(P)
, and
(I^{\prime},J^{\prime})=\mathit{WM}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}}(G)
.

We first show
I\subseteq I^{\prime}
, or equivalently

\displaystyle S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C})

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{G}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}\cup J^{\prime}).

Because
G^{\prime}\subseteq G
, we get

\displaystyle S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}\cup J^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{G}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}\cup J^{\prime}).

Because
\mathrm{pred}(B(P^{\prime})^{-})\cap E=\emptyset
, all rules
r\in G^{\prime}
satisfy
B(r)^{-}\cap\mathit{E\!C}\neq\emptyset
and
we obtain

\displaystyle S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}\cup J^{\prime}).

Because
\mathrm{pred}(H(P))\cap\mathrm{pred}(B(P)^{-})\subseteq E
and
\mathrm{pred}(B(P^{\prime})^{-})\cap\mathcal{E}=\emptyset
, all rules
r\in G^{\prime}
satisfy
B(r)^{-}\cap J^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
we obtain

\displaystyle S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C})

\displaystyle=S_{G^{\prime}}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{G}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{E\!C}\cup J^{\prime}).

To show
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
, we use
I\subseteq I^{\prime}
and
Proposition

14
:

\displaystyle S_{G}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I^{\prime})

\displaystyle\subseteq S_{G}^{\mathit{J\!C}}(\mathit{I\!C}\cup I).\qed

Proof of
Theorem

28
.

We begin by showing
\mathit{AM}({({P}_{j})}_{j\in\mathbb{J}})\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}(\pi(P))
and then show
\mathit{AM}({({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}})\leq_{p}\mathit{AM}({({P}_{j})}_{j\in\mathbb{J}})
.

Property
(\mathit{AM}({({P}_{j})}_{j\in\mathbb{J}})\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}(\pi(P)))
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{j}
,
(\mathit{I\!C}_{j},\mathit{J\!C}_{j})
, and
(I_{j},J_{j})

be defined as in
Equations

14
,
15
and
16

for the sequence
{({P}_{j})}_{j\in\mathbb{J}}
.
Similarly, let
{E}_{j}^{\prime}
,
(\mathit{I\!C}_{j}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}_{j}^{\prime})
, and
({I}_{j}^{\prime},{J}_{j}^{\prime})

be defined as in
Equations

5
,
4
and
3

for the sequence
{({G}_{j})}_{j\in\mathbb{J}}
with
{G}_{j}=\pi({P}_{j})
.
Furthermore, let
\mathit{E\!C}_{j}
be the set of all ground atoms over atoms in
\mathcal{E}_{j}
.

We first show
{E}_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{E\!C}_{j}
for each
j\in\mathbb{J}
by showing that
{E}_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{E\!C}_{j}
.
By
Lemma

60
, only negative body literals have to be taken into account:

\displaystyle{E}_{j}^{\prime}

\displaystyle={B({G}_{j})}^{\pm}\cap\bigcup_{j<k}H({G}_{k})

\displaystyle={B({G}_{j})}^{-}\cap\bigcup_{j<k}H({G}_{k}).

Observe that
\mathrm{pred}({B({G}_{j})}^{-}\subseteq\mathrm{pred}({B({P}_{j})}^{-}))
and
\mathrm{pred}(H({G}_{j}))\subseteq\mathrm{pred}(H({P}_{j}))
.
Thus, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{pred}({E}_{j}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{pred}({B({G}_{j})}^{-}\cap\bigcup_{j<k}H({G}_{k}))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{pred}({B({P}_{j})}^{-})\cap\mathrm{pred}(\bigcup_{j<k}H({P}_{k}))

\displaystyle\subseteq\mathrm{pred}({B({P}_{j})}^{-})\cap\mathrm{pred}(\bigcup_{j\leq k}H({P}_{k}))

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{j}.

It follows that
{E}_{j}^{\prime}\subseteq\mathit{E\!C}_{j}
.

By
Theorem

17
,
we have
\bigsqcup_{j\in\mathbb{J}}({I}_{j}^{\prime},{J}_{j}^{\prime})\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}(\pi(G))
.
To show the theorem, we show
(I_{j},J_{j})\leq_{p}({I}_{j}^{\prime},{J}_{j}^{\prime})
.
We omit the full induction proof and focus on the key idea:
Using
Theorem

27
, whose precondition holds by construction of
\mathcal{E}_{j}
, and
Proposition

15
, we get

\displaystyle\mathit{AM}^{\mathit{I\!C}_{j},\mathit{J\!C}_{j}}_{\mathcal{E}_{j}}({P}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{j},\mathit{J\!C}_{j})\sqcup(\emptyset,\mathit{E\!C}_{j})}({G}_{j})

\displaystyle\leq_{p}\mathit{WM}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{j}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}_{j}^{\prime})\sqcup(\emptyset,{E}_{j}^{\prime})}({G}_{j}).

Property
(\mathit{AM}({({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}})\leq_{p}\mathit{AM}({({P}_{i,j})}_{(i,j)\in\mathbb{J}}))
.

We omit a full induction proof for this property because it would be very technical.
Instead, we focus on the key idea why the approximate model of a refined instantiation sequence is at least as precise as the one of an instantiation sequence.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and
\mathcal{E}_{i,j}
be defined as in (
14
) for the instantiation and refined instantiation sequence, respectively.
Clearly, we have
\mathcal{E}_{i,j}\subseteq\mathcal{E}_{i}
for each
(i,j)\in\mathbb{J}
.
Observe, that (due to rule dependencies and
52

(c)
)
calculating the approximate model of the refined sequence,
using
\mathcal{E}_{i}
instead of
\mathcal{E}_{i,j}
in (
16
),
would result in the same approximate model as for the instantiation sequence.
With this, the property simply follows from the monotonicity of the stable operator.∎

Proof of
Theorem

29
.

Let
\mathcal{E}_{i}
,
(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})
, and
(I_{i},J_{i})

be defined as in
Equations

14
,
15
and
16

for the sequence
{({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
.
Similarly, let
{E}_{i}^{\prime}
,
(\mathit{I\!C}_{i}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}_{i}^{\prime})
, and
({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})

be defined as in
Equations

5
,
4
and
3

for the sequence
{({G}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}}
with
{G}_{i}=\pi({P}_{i})
.
Furthermore, we assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathbb{I}=\{1,\dots,n\}
.

We have already seen in the proof of
Theorem

28

that the atoms
{E}_{i}^{\prime}
are a subset of the ground atoms over predicates
\mathcal{E}_{i}
and that
(I_{i},J_{i})\leq_{p}({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})
.
Observing that ground atoms over predicates
\mathcal{E}_{i}
can only appear negatively in rule bodies,
we obtain that
{G}_{i}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}_{i}^{\prime})\cup({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})\cup(\emptyset,{E}_{i}^{\prime})}={G}_{i}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i}^{\prime},\mathit{J\!C}_{i}^{\prime})\cup({I}_{i}^{\prime},{J}_{i}^{\prime})}
.
By
Theorems

18
and
55
, we obtain that
\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{G}_{i}^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(I_{i},J_{i})}
and
\pi(P)
have the same well-founded and stable models.
To shorten the notation, we let

\displaystyle{F}_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi({P}_{i})^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(I_{i},J_{i})},

\displaystyle{H}_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cup J_{i}}({P}_{i})^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(I_{i},J_{i})},

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{F}_{i},\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{H}_{i}.

With this, it remains to show that programs
F
and
H
have the same well-founded and stable models.

We let
J=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}J_{i}
.
Furthermore, we let
\pi(a)
be a subformula in
{F}_{i}
and
\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cup J_{i}}(a)
be a subformula in
{H}_{i}

where both subformulas originate from the translation of the closed aggregate
a
.
(We see below that existence of one implies the existence of the other because both formulas are identical in their context.)

Because an aggregate always depends positively on the predicates occurring in its elements,
the intersection between
\bigcup_{i<k}H({F}_{k})=\bigcup_{i<k}J_{k}
and the atoms occurring in
\pi(a)
is empty.
Thus the two formulas
\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cup J_{i}}(a)
and
\pi_{J}(a)
are identical.
Observe that each stable model of either
F
and
H
is a subset of
J
.
By
Theorem

25
,
satisfiability of the aggregates formulas as well as their reducts is the same for subsets of
J
.
Thus, both formulas have the same stable models.
Similarly, the well-founded model of both formulas must be more-precise than
(\emptyset,J)
.
By
Theorem

25
,
satisfiability of the aggregate formulas as well as their
id
-reducts is the same.
Thus, both formulas have the same well-founded model.
∎

Proof of
Theorem

30
.

Clearly, we have
{\mathcal{E}}_{i}=\emptyset
if all components are stratified.
With this, the theorem follows from
Lemma

59
.
∎

We can characterize the result of
Algorithm

1
as follows.

Lemma 61
.

Let
r
be a safe normal rule,
(I,J)
be a finite four-valued interpretation,
f\in\{\textnormal{{t}},\textnormal{{f}}\}
, and
J^{\prime}
be a finite two-valued interpretation.

Then, a call to
\textnormal{{GroundRule}}^{I,J}_{r,f,J^{\prime}}(\iota,B(r))
returns the finite set of instances
g
of
r
satisfying

\displaystyle J\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I}\text{\ and }(f=\textnormal{{t}}\text{\ or\ }{B(g)}^{+}\nsubseteq J^{\prime}).

(25)

Proof.

Observe that the algorithm does not modify
f
,
r
,
(I,J)
, and
J^{\prime}
.
To shorten the notation below, let
G_{\sigma,L}=\textnormal{{GroundRule}}^{I,J}_{r,f,J^{\prime}}(\sigma,L)
.

Calling
G_{\iota,B(r)}
, the algorithm maintains the following invariants in subsequent calls
G_{\sigma,L}
:

\displaystyle{(B(r)\setminus L)}\sigma^{+}\subseteq J,

(I1)

\displaystyle{(B(r)\setminus L)}\sigma^{-}\cap I=\emptyset,\text{\ and\ }

(I2)

each comparison in
(B(r)\setminus L)\sigma
holds.

(I3)

We only prove the first invariant because the latter two can be shown in a similar way.
We prove by induction.

Base.

For the call
G_{\iota,B(r)}
, the invariant holds because the set difference
B(r)\setminus L
is empty for
L=B(r)
.

Hypothesis.

We assume the invariant holds for call
G_{\sigma,L}
and show that it is maintained in subsequent calls.

Step.

Observe that there are only further calls if
L
is non-empty.
In
Algorithm

1
, a body literal
l
is selected from
L
.
Observe that it is always possible to select such a literal.
In case that there are positive literals in
L
, we can select any one of them.
In case that there are no positive literals in
L
,

\sigma
replaces all variables in the positive body of
r
.
Because
r
is safe,
all literals in
L\sigma
are ground and we can select any one of them.

In case that
l
is a positive literal,
all substitutions
\sigma^{\prime}
, obtained by calling
\textnormal{{Matches}}^{I,J}_{l}(\sigma)
in the following line, ensure

\displaystyle l\sigma^{\prime}

\displaystyle\in J.

Furthermore,
\sigma
is more general than
\sigma^{\prime}
.
Thus, we have

\displaystyle{(B(r)\setminus L)\sigma^{\prime}}^{+}

\displaystyle={(B(r)\setminus L)\sigma}^{+}

\displaystyle\subseteq J.

In
Algorithm

1
, the algorithm calls
G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}
with
L^{\prime}=L\setminus\{l\}
.
We obtain

\displaystyle{(B(r)\setminus L^{\prime})\sigma^{\prime}}^{+}

\displaystyle={(B(r)\setminus L)\sigma^{\prime}}^{+}\cup\{l\sigma^{\prime}\}

\displaystyle\subseteq J.

In case that
l
is a comparison or negative literal,
we get
{(B(r)\setminus L)}^{+}={(B(r)\setminus L\setminus\{l\})}^{+}
.
Furthermore, the substitution
\sigma
is either not changed or is discarded altogether.
Thus, the invariant is maintained in subsequent calls to
GroundRule
.

We prove by induction over subsets
L
of
B(r)
with corresponding substitution
\sigma
satisfying invariants (
I1
)–(
I3
)
that
G_{L,\sigma}
is finite and that
g\in G_{L,\sigma}
iff
g
is a ground instance of
r\sigma
that satisfies (
25
).

Base.

We show the base case for
L=\emptyset
.
Using invariant (
I1
), we only have to consider substitutions
\sigma
with
{B(r)}^{+}\sigma\subseteq J
.
Because
r
is safe and
\sigma
replaces all variables in its positive body,

\sigma
also replaces all variables in its head and negative body.
Thus,
r\sigma
is ground and the remainder of the algorithm just filters the set
\{r\sigma\}

while the invariants (
I1
)–(
I3
) ensure that
J\models\tau(B(r\sigma))^{\wedge}_{I}
.
The condition in
Algorithm

1
cannot apply because
L=\emptyset
.
The condition in
Algorithm

1
discards rules
r\sigma
not satisfying
f=\textnormal{{t}}
or
{B(r\sigma)}^{+}\nsubseteq J^{\prime}
.

Hypothesis.

We show that the property holds for
L\neq\emptyset

assuming that it holds for subsets
L^{\prime}\subset L
with corresponding substitutions
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Step.

Because
L\neq\emptyset
we only have to consider the case in
Algorithm

1
.

First, the algorithm selects an element
l\in L
.
We have already seen that it is always possible to select such an element.
Let
L^{\prime}=L\setminus\{l\}
.
The algorithm then loops over the set

\displaystyle\Sigma

\displaystyle=\textnormal{{Matches}}^{I,J}_{l}(\sigma)

and, in
Algorithms

1
to
1
, computes the union

\displaystyle G_{\sigma,L}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma}G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}.

First, we show that the set
G_{\sigma,L}
is finite.
In case
l
is not a positive literal, the set
\Sigma
has at most one element.
In case
l
is a positive literal,
observe that there is a one-to-one correspondence between
\Sigma
and the set
\{l\sigma^{\prime}\mid\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma\}
.
We obtain that
\Sigma
is finite because
\{l\sigma^{\prime}\mid\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma\}\subseteq J
and
J
is finite.
Furthermore, using the induction hypothesis, each set
G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}
in the union
G_{\sigma,L}
is finite.
Hence, the set
G_{\sigma,L}
returned by the algorithm is finite.

Second, we show
g\in G_{\sigma,L}
implies that
g
is a ground instance of
r\sigma
satisfying (
25
).
We have that
g
is a member of some
G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}
.
By the induction hypothesis,
g
is a ground instance of
r\sigma^{\prime}
satisfying (
25
).
Observe that
g
is also a ground instance of
r\sigma
because
\sigma
is more general than
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Third, we show that each ground instance
g
of
r\sigma
satisfying (
25
) is also contained in
G_{\sigma,L}
.
Because
g
is a ground instance of
r\sigma
,
there is a substitution
\theta
more specific than
\sigma
such that
g=r\theta
.
In case that the selected literal
l\in L
is a positive literal,
we have
l\theta\in J
.
Then, there is also a substitution
\theta^{\prime}
such that
\theta^{\prime}\in\textnormal{{match}}(l\sigma,l\theta)
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}=\sigma\circ\theta^{\prime}
.
By
Definition

15
, we have
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Sigma
.
It follows that
g\in G_{\sigma,L}
because
g\in G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}
by the induction hypothesis and
G_{\sigma^{\prime},L^{\prime}}\subseteq G_{\sigma,L}
.
In the case that
l
is not a positive literal, we have
\sigma\in\Sigma
and can apply a similar argument.

Hence, we have shown that the proposition holds for
G_{\iota,B(r)}
.
∎

In terms of the program simplification in
Definition

1
,
the first condition in
Lemma

61
amounts to checking whether

H(g)\leftarrow\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}
is in
\tau(P)^{I,J}
,
which is the simplification of the (ground)
\mathcal{R}
-program
\tau(P)
preserving all stable models between
I
and
J
.
The two last conditions are meant to avoid duplicates from a previous invocation.
Since
r
is a normal rule, translation
\tau
is sufficient.

Proof of
Proposition

31
.

The first property directly follows from
Lemma

61
and the definition of
\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J}(\{r\})
.

It remains to show the second property.
Let
G
be the set of all ground instances of
r

and

\displaystyle G^{X,Y}_{f}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid Y\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I},(f=\textnormal{{t}}\text{\ or\ }{B(g)}^{+}\nsubseteq X)\}.

By
Lemma

61
and the first property, we can reformulate the second property of the proposition as
G^{\emptyset,J}_{\textnormal{{t}}}=G^{\emptyset,J^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{{t}}}\cup G^{J^{\prime},J}_{\textnormal{{f}}}
.
We have

\displaystyle G^{\emptyset,J}_{\textnormal{{t}}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid J\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I}\}

\displaystyle G^{\emptyset,J^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{{t}}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid J^{\prime}\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I}\}\text{, and}

\displaystyle G^{J^{\prime},J}_{\textnormal{{f}}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid J\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I},{B(g)}^{+}\nsubseteq J^{\prime}\}.

Observe that, given
J^{\prime}\subseteq J
, we can equivalently write
G^{\emptyset,J^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{{t}}}
as

\displaystyle G^{\emptyset,J^{\prime}}_{\textnormal{{t}}}

\displaystyle=\{g\in G\mid J\models\tau(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I},{B(g)}^{+}\subseteq J^{\prime}\}.

Because
{B(g)}^{+}\subseteq J^{\prime}
and
{B(g)}^{+}\nsubseteq J^{\prime}
cancel each other, we get

\displaystyle G_{\emptyset,J}

\displaystyle=G_{\emptyset,J^{\prime}}\cup G_{J^{\prime},J}.\qed

Proof of
Proposition

32
.

We first show
(a)
and then
(b)
.

Property
(a)
.

For a rule
r\in P
, we use
{r}^{\alpha}
to refer to the corresponding rule with replaced aggregate occurrences in
{P}^{\alpha}
.
Similarly, for a ground instance
g
of
r
, we use
{g}^{\alpha}
to to refer to the corresponding instance of
{r}^{\alpha}
.
Observe that
\pi_{J}(P)^{I,J}=\pi_{J}(\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J}(P))
.
We show that
g\in\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J}(P)
iff
{g}^{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J\cup\mathit{J\!A}}({P}^{\alpha})
.
In the following, because the rule bodies of
g
and
