{g}^{\alpha}
only differ regarding aggregates and their replacement atoms, we only consider rules with aggregates in their bodies.

Case
g\in\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J}(P)
.

Let
r
be a rule in
P
containing aggregate
a
,

\alpha
be the replacement atom of form (
20
) for
a
, and

\sigma
be a ground substitution such that
r\sigma=g
.
We show that for each aggregate
a\sigma\in B(g)
, we have

\epsilon_{r,a}({G}^{\epsilon},\sigma)\cup G\neq\emptyset
and
J\models{\pi_{G}}(a\sigma)_{I}

with
G=\eta_{r,a}({G}^{\eta},\sigma)

and in turn
\mathit{J\!A}\models\alpha\sigma
.
Because
J\models\pi(B(g))^{\wedge}_{I}
,
we get
J\models{\pi_{J}}(a\sigma)_{I}
.
It remains to show that
\epsilon_{r,a}({G}^{\epsilon},\sigma)\cup G\neq\emptyset
and
{\pi_{J}}(a\sigma)={\pi_{G}}(a\sigma)
.
Observe that
{\pi_{J}}(a\sigma)={\pi_{G}}(a\sigma)

because the set
G
obtained from rules in
{G}^{\eta}
contains all instances of elements of
a\sigma

whose conditions are satisfied by
J

while the remaining literals of these rules are contained in the body of
g
.
Furthermore,
observe that if no aggregate element is satisfied by
J
,
we get
\epsilon_{r,a}({G}^{\epsilon},\sigma)\neq\emptyset
because the corresponding ground instance of
Equation

21
is satisfied.

Case
{g}^{\alpha}\in\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J\cup\mathit{J\!A}}({P}^{\alpha})
.

Let
{r}^{\alpha}
be a rule in
{P}^{\alpha}
containing replacement atom
\alpha
of form (
20
) for aggregate
a
and

\sigma
be a ground substitution such that
{r}^{\alpha}\sigma={g}^{\alpha}
.
Because
J\cup\mathit{J\!A}\models\tau(B({g}^{\alpha}))^{\wedge}_{I}
, we have
\alpha\sigma\in\mathit{J\!A}
.
Thus, we get that
J\models{\pi_{G}}(a\sigma)_{I}
with
G=\eta_{r,a}({G}^{\eta},\sigma)
.
We have already seen in the previous case that
{\pi_{J}}(a\sigma)={\pi_{G}}(a\sigma)
.
Thus,
J\models{\pi_{J}}(a\sigma)_{I}
.
Observing that
g=r\sigma
and
a\sigma\in B(g)
, we get
g\in\mathrm{Inst}^{I,J}(P)
.

Property
(b)
.

This property follows from
(a)
,
25
and
48
.∎

Proof of
Proposition

33
.

We prove
(a)
and
(b)
by showing that the function calculates the stable model by iteratively calling the
T
operator until a fixed-point is reached.

Property
(a)
and
(b)
.

At each iteration
i
of the loop starting with 1,
let
\mathit{J\!A}_{i}
be the value of
\textnormal{{Propagate}}_{P}^{I,J}({G}^{\epsilon},{G}^{\eta})
in
Algorithm

2
,

{G}^{\epsilon}_{i}
,
{G}^{\eta}_{i}
, and
{G}^{\alpha}_{i}
be the values on the right-hand-side of the assignments in
Algorithms

2
,
2
and
2
, and

J_{i}=H({G}^{\alpha}_{i})
.
Furthermore, let
J_{0}=\emptyset
.

By
Proposition

31
, we get

\displaystyle{G}^{\epsilon}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inst}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup J_{i-1}}({P}^{\epsilon}),

\displaystyle{G}^{\eta}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inst}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup J_{i-1}}({P}^{\eta}),

\displaystyle\mathit{J\!A}_{i}

\displaystyle=\textnormal{{Propagate}}_{P}^{I,J}({G}^{\epsilon}_{i},{G}^{\eta}_{i})\text{, and\ }

\displaystyle{G}^{\alpha}_{i}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Inst}^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!A}_{i}\cup J_{i-1}}({P}^{\alpha}).

Using
Proposition

32

(b)
and
observing the one-to-one correspondence between
{G}^{\alpha}_{i}
and
\pi(P)^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup\mathit{J\!C}_{i}}
, we get

\displaystyle J_{i}

\displaystyle=H({G}^{\alpha}_{i})

\displaystyle=T_{\pi(P)_{\mathit{I\!C}}}(\mathit{J\!C}\cup J_{i-1}).

Observe that, if the loop exits, then the algorithm computes the fixed point of
T_{\pi(P)_{\mathit{I\!C}}}^{\mathit{J\!C}}
, i.e.,

J=S_{\pi(P)}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C})
.
Furthermore, observe that this fixed point calculation terminates whenever
S_{\pi(P)}^{\mathit{I\!C}}(\mathit{J\!C})
is finite.
Finally, we obtain
\textnormal{{GroundComponent}}(P,\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C})=\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}\cup J}(P)^{\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}\cup J}
using
Proposition

32

(a)
.

Property
(c)
.

We have seen above that the interpretation
J
is a fixed point of
T_{\pi(P)_{\mathit{I\!C}}}^{\mathit{J\!C}}
.
By
Proposition

32

(b)
and observing that function
Assemble
only modifies rule bodies,
we get
H(\textnormal{{GroundComponent}}(P,\mathit{I\!C},\mathit{J\!C}))=J
.∎

Proof of
Theorem

34
.

Since the program is finite, its instantiation sequences are finite, too.
We assume w.l.o.g. that
\mathbb{I}=\{1,\dots,n\}
for some
n\geq 0
.
We let
\mathit{F\!C}_{i}
and
\mathit{G\!C}_{i}
be the values of variables
F
and
G
at iteration
i
at the beginning of the loop in
Algorithms

3
to
3
,
and
{F}_{i}
and
{G}_{i}
be the results of the calls to
GroundComponent
in
Algorithms

3
and
3
at iteration
i
.

By
Proposition

33
,
we get that
Algorithms

3
to
3
correspond to an application of the approximate model operator
as given in
Definition

13
.
For each iteration
i
, we get

\displaystyle(\mathit{F\!C}_{i},\mathit{G\!C}_{i})

\displaystyle=\bigsqcup_{j<i}({F}_{i},{G}_{i}),

\displaystyle(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})

\displaystyle=(H(\mathit{F\!C}_{i}),H(\mathit{G\!C}_{i})),

\displaystyle(I_{i},J_{i})

\displaystyle=(H({F}_{i}),H({G}_{i}))\text{, and\ }

\displaystyle{G}_{i}

\displaystyle=\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cup J_{i}}({P}_{i})^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(I_{i},J_{i})}

whenever
(I_{i},J_{i})
is finite.
In case that each
(I_{i},J_{i})
is finite, the algorithm returns
in
Algorithm

3
the program

\displaystyle\mathit{G\!C}_{n}\cup{G}_{n}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}{G}_{i}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{I}}\pi_{\mathit{J\!C}_{i}\cup J_{i}}({P}_{i})^{(\mathit{I\!C}_{i},\mathit{J\!C}_{i})\sqcup(I_{i},J_{i})}.

Thus, the algorithm terminates iff each call to
GroundComponent
is finite,
which is exactly the case when
\mathit{AM}({({P}_{i})}_{i\in\mathbb{I}})
is finite.
∎

Proof of
Corollary

35
.

This is a direct consequence of
Theorems

29
and
34
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

36
.

Let
G
be the set of ground instances of the aggregate elements of
a

and
D\subseteq G
be a set such that
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a
.

Due to the antimonotonicity of the aggregate, we get
{\pi_{a}}(D)=\bot
.
Thus, the reduct is constant because all consequents in
\pi(a)
as well as
\pi(a)_{I}
are equal to
\bot
and the antecedents in
\pi(a)
are completely evaluated by the reduct.
Hence, the lemma follows by
Lemma

44

(b)
.∎

Proof of
Proposition

37
.

We only show
(a)
because the proof of
(b)
is symmetric.

Let
a=\mathrm{\#sum}\{E\}\succ b
and
a_{+}=\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}\{E\}\succ b^{\prime}
.
Given an arbitrary two-valued interpretation
J
, we consider the following two cases:

Case
J\not\models\pi(a)_{I}
.

There is a set
D\subseteq G
such that
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a
,
I\models\tau(D)^{\wedge}
, and
J\not\models{\pi_{a}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Let
\widehat{D}=D\cup\{e\in G\mid I\models\tau(e),w(H(e))<0\}
.

Clearly,
\widehat{D}\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a
and
I\models\tau(\widehat{D})^{\wedge}
.
Furthermore,
J\not\models{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}

because we constructed
\widehat{D}
so that
{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
is stronger than
{\pi_{a}}(D)^{\vee}

because more elements with negative weights have to be taken into considerations.

Next, observe that
\widehat{D}\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{+}
holds
because we have
\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(H(\widehat{D}))=\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(H(D))
and
\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(H(\widehat{D}))=\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T)
,
which corresponds to the value subtracted from the bound of
a_{+}
.
To show that
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
,
we show
{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
is stronger than
{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
.
Let
C\subseteq G\setminus\widehat{D}
be a set of elements such that
\widehat{D}\cup C\triangleright a_{+}
.
Because the justification of
a_{+}
is independent of elements with negative weights,
each clause in
{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
involving an element with a negative weight is subsumed by another clause without that element.
Thus, we only consider sets
C
containing elements with positive weights.
Observe that
\widehat{D}\cup C\triangleright a
holds
because we have
\mathrm{\#sum}(H(\widehat{D}\cup C))=\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(H(\widehat{D}\cup C))+\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T)
.
Hence, we get
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
.

Case
J\not\models\pi(a_{+})_{I}
.

There is a set
D\subseteq G
such that
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{+}
,
I\models\tau(D)^{\wedge}
, and
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{+}}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Let
\widehat{D}=D\cup\{e\in G\mid I\models\tau(e),w(H(e))<0\}
.

Observe that
\widehat{D}\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{+}
,
I\models\tau(\widehat{D})^{\wedge}
, and
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
.
As in the previous case, we can show that
{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
is stronger than
{\pi_{a_{+}}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}

because clauses in
{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
involving elements with negative weights are subsumed.
Hence, we get
J\not\models{\pi_{a}}(\widehat{D})^{\vee}
.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

38
.

Let
G
be the set of ground instances of
E
,

a_{\prec}=f\{E\}\prec b
for aggregate relation
{\prec}
,
and
J
be a two-valued interpretation.

Property
(a)
.

We show that

J\models\pi(a_{<})_{I}\vee\pi(a_{>})_{I}
implies

J\models\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
.

Case
J\models\pi(a_{<})_{I}
.

Observe that
\pi(a_{\neq})
is conjunction of implications of form

{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}\rightarrow{\pi_{a_{\neq}}}(D)^{\vee}
with
D\subseteq G
and
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\neq}
.
Furthermore, note that
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\neq}
implies
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{<}
.
Thus,
\pi(a_{<})
contains the implication
{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}\rightarrow{\pi_{a_{<}}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Because
J\models\pi(a_{<})_{I}
, we get
I\not\models{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}
or
J\models{\pi_{a_{<}}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Hence, the property holds in this case because
J\models{\pi_{a_{<}}}(D)^{\vee}
implies
J\models{\pi_{a_{\neq}}}(D)^{\vee}
.

Case
J\models\pi(a_{>})_{I}
.

The property can be shown analogously for this case.

Property
(a)
.

This property can be shown in a similar way as the previous one.
We show by contraposition that

J\models\pi(a_{=})_{I}

implies

J\models\pi(a_{\leq})_{I}\wedge\pi(a_{\geq})_{I}
.

Case
J\not\models\pi(a_{\leq})_{I}
.

Observe that
\pi(a_{\leq})
is conjunction of implications of form

{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}\rightarrow{\pi_{a_{\leq}}}(D)^{\vee}
with
D\subseteq G
and
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\leq}
.
Furthermore, note that
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\leq}
implies
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{=}
.
Thus,
\pi(a_{=})
contains the implication
{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}\rightarrow{\pi_{a_{=}}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Because
J\not\models\pi(a_{\leq})_{I}
, we get
I\models{\tau(D)}^{\wedge}
and
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{<}}}(D)^{\vee}

for some
D\subseteq G
with
D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\leq}
.
Hence, the property holds in this case because
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{\leq}}}(D)^{\vee}
implies
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{=}}}(D)^{\vee}
.

Case
J\not\models\pi(a_{\geq})_{I}
.

The property can be shown analogously for this case. ∎

Proof of
Proposition

39
.

We only consider the case that
f
is the
\mathrm{\#sum}
function because the other ones are special cases of this function.
Furthermore, we only consider the only if directions because we have already established the other directions in
Proposition

38
.

Let
G
be the set of ground instances of
E
,

T_{I}=H(\{g\in G\mid I\models B(g)\})
, and

T_{J}=H(\{g\in G\mid J\models B(g)\})
.

Property
(a)
.

Because
I\subseteq J
, we get
\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})
and
\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})
.
We prove by contraposition.

Case
J\not\models\pi(a_{<})_{I}
and
J\not\models\pi(a_{>})_{I}
.

We use
Propositions

24
and
37
to get the following two inequalities:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\geq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})\text{\ because\ $J\not\models\pi(a_{<})_{I}$ and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\leq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})\text{\ because\ $J\not\models\pi(a_{>})_{I}$.}

Using
\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})
, we can rearrange as

\displaystyle b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\leq{}\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})

\displaystyle\leq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J}).

Using
\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J}).

Using
\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})=\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})
, we get

\displaystyle b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})

\displaystyle\leq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})

and, thus, obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}(T_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}(T_{J}).

Observe that this gives rise to an implication in
\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
that is not satisfied by
J
.
Hence, we get
J\not\models\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
.

Property
(b)
.

Because
J\subseteq I
, we get
\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})
and
\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})\leq\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})
.

Case
J\models\pi(a_{\leq})_{I}
and
J\models\pi(a_{\geq})_{I}
.

Using
Propositions

24
and
37
, we get

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\geq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})\text{\ because\ $J\models\pi(a_{\geq})_{I}$ and}

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})

\displaystyle\leq b-\mathrm{\#sum}^{+}(T_{I})\text{\ because\ $J\models\pi(a_{\leq})_{I}$.}

Observe that we can proceed as in the proof of the previous property because the relation symbols are just flipped.
We obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}^{-}(T_{J})\text{\ and\ }

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}(T_{I})

\displaystyle=\mathrm{\#sum}(T_{J}).

We get
J\models\pi(a_{=})_{I}

because for any subset of tuples in
T_{I}
that do not satisfy the aggregate,
we have additional tuples in
T_{J}
that satisfy the aggregate.
∎

Proof of
Proposition

40
.

Let
a_{\prec}=f\{E\}\prec b
for
{\prec}\in\{{=},{\neq}\}
.

Property
(a)
.

We prove by contraposition that
J\models\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
implies that
there is no set
X\subseteq T_{I}
such that
f(X\cup T_{J})=b
.

Case there is a set
X\subseteq T_{I}
such that
f(X\cup T_{J})=b
.

Let
D=\{e\in G\mid I\models B(e),H(e)\in X\cup T_{J}\}
.
Because
T_{J}\subseteq T_{I}

D\mathrel{\not\kern-1.0pt\triangleright}a_{\neq}
.
Furthermore, we have
I\models\tau(D)^{\wedge}
.
Observe that
D
contains all elements with conditions satisfied by
J
.
Hence, we get
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{\neq}}}(D)^{\vee}
and, in turn,
J\not\models\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
.

We prove the remaining direction, again, by contraposition.

Case
J\not\models\pi(a_{\neq})_{I}
.

There is a set
D\subseteq G
such that
I\models\tau(D)^{\wedge}
and
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{\neq}}}(D)^{\vee}
.
Let
X=H(D)
.
Because
J\not\models{\pi_{a_{\neq}}}(D)^{\vee}
, we get
f(X\cup T_{J})=b
.

Property
(b)
.

This property can be shown in a similar way as the previous one.∎
